There’s this part of me that knows I should, like I’m watching myself and thinking, “Joan, stop,” but my hands just keep opening more food and I keep eating until I’m painfully full.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating a large amount of food happen regularly or is this a one-time thing?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not a one-time thing. For the past few months, I don’t think there’s really been a week where it didn’t happen at least once. And then the next day I usually try to make up for it, like by not eating much at school or making myself do a long walk or extra yoga.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to describe it. I know I feel bad afterward, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt or shame exactly. It’s more like I just wish it hadn’t happened, and I don’t want to think about it too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really want to say yes or no because I’m not sure how to answer that. I know I’ve tried to make up for it afterward in other ways, but vomiting isn’t something I can clearly say is part of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to counteract the effects of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really think of laxatives or water pills as part of what I do afterward. I mean, I’ve mostly just tried to make up for it in other ways, but I can’t say I’ve noticed using those specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically eat these large amounts of food in a short period of time without trying to make up for it afterward?\n\nPatient: Not really. I usually do try to make up for it. Like after one of those nights, I’ll tell myself I’m not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school, and then I’ll go stay out on the trail for hours or do extra yoga, even if I’m shaky and completely exhausted.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these episodes of eating a large amount of food in one sitting?\n\nPatient: For the past few months, it’s been pretty much every week. I don’t think there’s been many weeks without at least one night where I eat way more than I meant to in one sitting.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to keep from gaining weight when you do things like extra yoga or not eating the next day after one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, that’s the main reason I do it. After I eat like that, I feel like I have to stop myself from gaining weight, so I’ll skip breakfast or lunch, or stay out on the trail for hours, or do extra yoga even when I’m shaky and worn out.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that. I haven’t been focusing on that part, I guess. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you consider your actions after these episodes, like extra yoga or skipping meals, to be a way to make up for the calories you consumed?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s what it is. After one of those nights, I’ll tell myself I’m not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school, and then I’ll stay out on the trail for hours or do extra yoga, even if I’m shaky and exhausted. It’s basically me trying to make up for what I ate.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I usually do try to make up for it. I mean, that’s the main reason I do it. After I eat like that, I feel like I have to stop myself from gaining weight, so I’ll skip breakfast or lunch, or stay out on the trail for hours, or do extra yoga even when I’m shaky and worn out.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that. I haven’t been focusing on that part, I guess. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you consider your actions after these episodes, like extra yoga or skipping meals, to be a way to make up for the calories you consumed?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s what it is. After one of those nights, I’ll tell myself I’m not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school, and then I’ll stay out on the trail for hours or do extra yoga, even if I’m shaky and exhausted. It’s basically me trying to make up for what I ate.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I know I do things afterward to try to make up for it, but with vomiting specifically… I don’t really know how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever intentionally made yourself vomit after eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. It scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these nights?\n\nPatient: Yes. Once I start, it feels like I can’t stop. There’s this part of me that knows I should, like I’m watching myself and thinking, “Joan, stop,” but my hands just keep opening more food and I keep eating until I’m painfully full.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating a large amount of food happen regularly or is this a one-time thing?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not a one-time thing. For the past few months, I don’t think there’s really been a week where it didn’t happen at least once. And then the next day I usually try to make up for it, like by not eating much at school or making myself do a long walk or extra yoga.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to describe it. I know I feel bad afterward, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt or shame exactly. It’s more like I just wish it hadn’t happened, and I don’t want to think about it too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really want to say yes or no because I’m not sure how to answer that. I know I’ve tried to make up for it afterward in other ways, but vomiting isn’t something I can clearly say is part of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to counteract the effects of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really think of laxatives or water pills as part of what I do afterward. I usually do try to make up for it. Like after one of those nights, I’ll tell myself I’m not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school, and then I’ll go stay out on the trail for hours or do extra yoga, even if I’m shaky and completely exhausted.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these episodes of eating a large amount of food in one sitting?\n\nPatient: For the past few months, it’s been pretty much every week. I don’t think there’s been many weeks without at least one night where I eat way more than I meant to in one sitting.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to keep from gaining weight when you do things like extra yoga or not eating the next day after one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep having these nights where I eat way more than feels normal. Like after I’m done grading papers, I’ll start with leftovers, and then it turns into cereal, crackers, peanut butter, whatever snacks I packed for my classroom… just a really large amount of food. It scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these nights?\n\nPatient: Yes. Once I start, it feels like I can’t stop. There’s this part of me that knows I should, like I’m watching myself and thinking, “Joan, stop,” but my hands just keep opening more food and I keep eating until I’m painfully full.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating a large amount of food happen regularly or is this a one-time thing?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not a one-time thing. For the past few months, I don’t think there’s really been a week where it didn’t happen at least once. And then the next day I usually try to make up for it, like by not eating much at school or making myself do a long walk or extra yoga.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to describe it. I know I feel bad afterward, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt or shame exactly. It’s more like I just wish it hadn’t happened, and I don’t want to think about it too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really want to say yes or no because I’m not sure how to answer that. I know I’ve tried to make up for it afterward in other ways, but vomiting isn’t something I can clearly say is part of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to counteract the effects of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really think of laxatives or water pills as part of what I do afterward. I mean, I’ve mostly just tried to make up for it in other ways, but I can’t say I’ve noticed using those specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically eat these large amounts of food in a short period of time without trying to make up for it afterward?\n\nPatient: Not really. I usually do try to make up for it. Like after one of those nights, I’ll tell myself I’m not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school, and then I’ll go stay out on the trail for hours or do extra yoga, even if I’m shaky and completely exhausted.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these episodes of eating a large amount of food in one sitting?\n\nPatient: For the past few months, it’s been pretty much every week. I don’t think there’s been many weeks without at least one night where I eat way more than I meant to in one sitting.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to keep from gaining weight when you do things like extra yoga or not eating the next day after one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, that’s the main reason I do it. After I eat like that, I feel like I have to stop myself from gaining weight, so I’ll skip breakfast or lunch, or stay out on the trail for hours, or do extra yoga even when I’m shaky and worn out.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that. I haven’t been focusing on that part, I guess. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you consider your actions after these episodes, like extra yoga or skipping meals, to be a way to make up for the calories you consumed?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s what it is. After one of those nights, I’ll tell myself I’m not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school, and then I’ll stay out on the trail for hours or do extra yoga, even if I’m shaky and exhausted. It’s basically me trying to make up for what I ate.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I know I do things afterward to try to make up for it, but with vomiting specifically… I don’t really know how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever intentionally made yourself vomit after eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I can talk about other things I do afterward, but with making myself throw up on purpose… I’m not sure how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent weight gain when you engage in these compensatory behaviors after eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s the reason I do it. I know there are things I do afterward to make up for it, but saying I made myself vomit on purpose… I don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever intentionally made yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I’m having a hard time saying yes or no to that. I haven’t been focusing on that part, I guess. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you consider your actions after these episodes, like extra yoga or skipping meals, to be a way to make up for the calories you consumed?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s what it is. After one of those nights, I’ll tell myself I’m not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school, and then I’ll stay out on the trail for hours or do extra yoga, even if I’m shaky and exhausted. It’s basically me trying to make up for what I ate.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I know I do things afterward to try to make up for it, but with vomiting specifically… I don’t really know how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever intentionally made yourself vomit after eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I can talk about other things I do afterward, but with making myself throw up on purpose… I’m not sure how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent weight gain when you engage in these compensatory behaviors after eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s the reason I do it. After I eat like that, I feel like I have to stop the weight gain somehow, so I’ll skip breakfast or lunch, or I’ll stay out on the trail for hours or do extra yoga even when I feel shaky and worn out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used vomiting as a way to try to make up for eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I know I try to make up for it in other ways, but with vomiting specifically, I don’t really know how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit on purpose after one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I’m still not really sure how to answer that. I know there are things I do afterward to make up for it, but saying I made myself vomit on purpose… I don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever intentionally made yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I’m having a hard time saying yes or no to that. I know I’ve tried to make up for it afterward in other ways, but vomiting isn’t something I can clearly say is part of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to counteract the effects of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really think of laxatives or water pills as part of what I do afterward. I mean, I’ve mostly just tried to make up for it in other ways, but I can’t say I’ve noticed using those specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically eat these large amounts of food in a short period of time without trying to make up for it afterward?\n\nPatient: Not really. I usually do try to make up for it. Like after one of those nights, I’ll tell myself I’m not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school, and then I’ll go stay out on the trail for hours or do extra yoga, even if I’m shaky and completely exhausted.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these episodes of eating a large amount of food in one sitting?\n\nPatient: For the past few months, it’s been pretty much every week. I don’t think there’s been many weeks without at least one night where I eat way more than I meant to in one sitting.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to keep from gaining weight when you do things like extra yoga or not eating the next day after one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, that’s the main reason I do it. After I eat like that, I feel like I have to stop myself from gaining weight, so I’ll skip breakfast or lunch, or stay out on the trail for hours, or do extra yoga even when I’m shaky and worn out.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that. I haven’t been focusing on that part, I guess. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you consider your actions after these episodes, like extra yoga or skipping meals, to be a way to make up for the calories you consumed?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s what it is. After one of those nights, I’ll tell myself I’m not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school, and then I’ll stay out on the trail for hours or do extra yoga, even if I’m shaky and exhausted. It’s basically me trying to make up for what I ate.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I know I do things afterward to try to make up for it, but with vomiting specifically… I don’t really know how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever intentionally made yourself vomit after eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I can talk about other things I do afterward, but with making myself throw up on purpose… I’m not sure how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent weight gain when you engage in these compensatory behaviors after eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s the reason I do it. After I eat like that, I feel like I have to stop the weight gain somehow, so I’ll skip breakfast or lunch, or I’ll stay out on the trail for hours or do extra yoga even when I feel shaky and worn out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used vomiting as a way to try to make up for eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I know I try to make up for it in other ways, but with vomiting specifically, I don’t really know how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit on purpose after one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I’m still not really sure how to answer that. I know there are things I do afterward to make up for it, but saying I made myself vomit on purpose… I don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever intentionally made yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I’m having a hard time saying yes or no to that. I know I do other things after eating, but with making myself vomit intentionally, I’m just not sure how to answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used vomiting as a way to try to make up for eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I really don’t know how to answer that.
